mantine-vt8288" type="button" data-component="copy-link"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"><path d="M3.9 12c0-1.71 1.39-3.1 3.1-3.1h4V7H7c-2.76 0-5 2.24-5 5s2.24 5 5 5h4v-1.9H7c-1.71 0-3.1-1.39-3.1-3.1M8 13h8v-2H8zm9-6h-4v1.9h4c1.71 0 3.1 1.39 3.1 3.1s-1.39 3.1-3.1 3.1h-4V17h4c2.76 0 5-2.24 5-5s-2.24-5-5-5"></path></svg></button></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><div class="mantine-Grid-col mantine-3816o2"><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-i7qrnu" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>We recently came across a stealer, called <code>Raccoon Stealer,</code> a name given to it by its author. Raccoon Stealer uses the <code>Telegram</code> infrastructure to store and update actual <code>C&amp;C</code> addresses.&nbsp;</p><p>Raccoon Stealer is a password stealer capable of stealing not just passwords, but various types of data, including:</p><ul><li>Cookies, saved logins and forms data from browsers</li><li>Login credentials from email clients and messengers</li><li>Files from crypto wallets</li><li>Data from browser plugins and extension</li><li>Arbitrary files based on commands from C&amp;C</li></ul><p>In addition, it’s able to download and execute arbitrary files by command from its C&amp;C. In combination with active development and promotion on underground forums, Raccoon Stealer is prevalent and dangerous.</p><p>The oldest samples of Raccoon Stealer we’ve seen have timestamps from the <code>end of April 2019</code>. Its authors have stated the same month as the start of selling the malware on underground forums. Since then, it has been updated many times. According to its authors, they fixed bugs, added features, and more.</p><h2><strong>Distribution</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen Raccoon distributed via downloaders: <code>Buer Loader</code> and <code>GCleaner</code>. According to some samples, we believe it is also being distributed in the form of <code>fake game cheats</code>, <code>patches for cracked software</code> (including hacks and mods for <code>Fortnite</code>, <code>Valorant</code>, and <code>NBA2K22</code>), or other software. Taking into account that Raccoon Stealer is for sale, it’s distribution techniques are limited only by the imagination of the end buyers. Some samples are spread unpacked, while some are protected using <code>Themida</code> or malware packers. Worth noting is that some samples were packed more than <a href="https://twitter.com/AvastThreatLabs/status/1420372556142432257">five times in a row</a> with the same packer!&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Technical details</strong></p><p>Raccoon Stealer is written in <code>C/C++</code> and built using <code>Visual Studio</code>. Samples have a size of about <code>580-600 kB</code>. The code quality is below average, some strings are encrypted, some are not.</p><p>Once executed, Racoon Stealer starts checking for the default user locale set on the infected device and won’t work if it’s one of the following:</p><ul><li>Russian</li><li>Ukrainian</li><li>Belarusian</li><li>Kazakh</li><li>Kyrgyz</li><li>Armenian</li><li>Tajik</li><li>Uzbek</li></ul><h2><strong>C&amp;C communications</strong></h2><p>The most interesting thing about this stealer is its communication with C&amp;Cs. There are four values crucial for its C&amp;C communication, which are hardcoded in every Raccoon Stealer sample:</p><ul><li><code>MAIN_KEY</code>. This value has been changed four times during the year.</li><li><code>URLs</code> of Telegram gates with channel name. Gates are used not to implement a complicated Telegram protocol and not to store any credentials inside samples</li><li><code>BotID</code> – hexadecimal string, sent to the C&amp;C every time</li><li><code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> – a key to decrypt the C&amp;C address obtained from Telegram Gate</li></ul><p>Let’s look at an example to see how it works:<br><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code> unpacked to:<br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code>:</p><ol><li>First of all, <code>MAIN_KEY</code> is decrypted. See the decryption code in the image below:</li></ol></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=e4-pfmvQ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=-APkV-57 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=tYXHOcuC 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=hgb3xobM 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=kRbsgrUW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/rs00.png.webp?itok=lGB2Xpgk 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="mantine-q24tb8" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs00.png.webp%3Fitok%3DkRbsgrUW&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs00.png.webp%3Fitok%3DkRbsgrUW&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs00.png.webp%3Fitok%3DkRbsgrUW&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>In this example, the <code>MAIN_KEY</code> is <code>jY1aN3zZ2j</code>. to finally decrypt the C&amp;C URL: <code>http://91.219.236[.]18/</code></p><ol start="4"><li>Raccoon makes a query string with PC information (machine GUID and user name), and <code>BotID</code></li><li>Query string is encrypted with <code>RC4</code> using a <code>MAIN_KEY</code> and then encoded with Base64.</li><li>This data is sent using POST to the C&amp;C, and the response is encoded with Base64 and encrypted with the <code>MAIN_KEY</code>. class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Thus, the Telegram infrastructure is used to store and update actual C&amp;C addresses. It looks quite convenient and reliable until Telegram decides to take action.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Analysis</strong></p><h2><strong>The people behind Raccoon Stealer</strong></h2><p>Based on our analysis of seller messages on underground forums, we can deduce some information about the people behind the malware. Raccoon Stealer was developed by a team, some (or maybe all) members of the team are Russian native speakers. Messages on the forum are written in Russian, and we assume they are from former USSR countries because they try to prevent the Stealer from targeting users in these countries.</p><p>Possible names/nicknames of group members may be supposed based on the analysis of artifacts, found in samples:</p><ul><li><code>C:\Users\a13xuiop1337\</code></li><li><code>C:\Users\David\&nbsp;</code></li></ul><h2><strong>Prevalence</strong></h2><p>Raccoon Stealer is quite prevalent: from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code> our systems detected <code>more than 25,000</code> Raccoon-related samples. We identified <code>more than 1,300</code> distinct configs during that period.<br>&nbsp;</p><p>Here is a map, showing the number of systems Avast protected from Raccoon Stealer from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code>. In this time frame, Avast protected nearly <code>600,000</code> Raccoon Stealer attacks.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/rs06.png.webp?itok=IM2ZtqeK 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs06.png.webp%3Fitok%3DL7c9xtm9&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The country where we have blocked the most attempts is Russia, which is interesting because the actors behind the malware don’t want to infect computers in Russia or Central Asia. We believe the attacks spray and pray, distributing the malware around the world. It’s not until it makes it onto a system that it begins checking for the default locale. If it is one of the language listed above, it won’t run. This explains why we detected so many attack attempts in Russia, we block the malware before it can run, ie. before it can even get to the stage where it checks for the device’s locale. If an unprotected device that comes across the malware with its locale set to English or any other language that is not on the exception list but is in Russia, it would stiIl become infected.&nbsp;</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/rs07.png.webp?itok=j8AiQ7xC 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/rs07.png.webp?itok=glzTD6Ku 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/rs07.png.webp?itok=Weo3ciaf 1x, 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs07.png.webp%3Fitok%3DOOq5jBnf&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Screenshot with claims about not working with CIS</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Telegram Channels</strong></h2><p>From the more than 1,300 distinct configs we extracted, 429 of them are unique Telegram channels. Some of them were used only in a single config, others were used dozens of times. The most used channels were:</p><ul><li><code>jdiamond13</code> – 122 times</li><li><code>jjbadb0y</code> – 44 times</li><li><code>nixsmasterbaks2</code> – 31 times</li><li><code>hellobyegain</code> – 25 times</li><li><code>h_smurf1kman_1&nbsp;</code> – 24 times</li></ul><p>Thus, five of the most used channels were found in about <code>19%</code> of configs.</p><h2><strong>Malware distributed by Raccoon</strong></h2><p>As was previously mentioned, Raccoon Stealer is able to download and execute arbitrary files from a command from C&amp;C. We managed to collect some of these files. We collected <code>185 files</code>, with a total size <code>265 Mb</code>, and some of the groups are:</p><ul><li><code>Downloaders</code> – used to download and execute other files</li><li><code>Clipboard crypto stealers</code> – change crypto wallet addresses in the clipboard – very popular (more than 10%)</li><li><code>WhiteBlackCrypt Ransomware</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Servers used to download this software</strong></h3><p>We extracted unique links to other malware from Raccoon configs received from C&amp;Cs, it was <code>196 unique URLs</code>. Some analysis results:</p><ul><li><code>43% </code>of URLs have <code>HTTP</code> scheme, <code>57%</code> – <code>HTTPS</code>.</li><li><code>83 domain names</code> were used.</li><li>About <code>20% of malware</code> were placed on <code>Discord CDN</code></li><li>About <code>10%</code> were served from <code>aun3xk17k[.]space</code></li></ul><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We will continue to monitor Raccoon Stealer’s activity, keeping an eye on new C&amp;Cs, Telegram channels, and downloaded samples. We predict it may be used wider by other cybercrime groups. We assume the group behind Raccoon Stealer will further develop new features, including new software to steal data from, for example, as well as bypass protection this software has in place.</p><h2><strong>IoC</strong></h2><p><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code><br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code></p></div></div><div problem: Same skill, different hash\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs we developed the Agent Trust Hub (ATH)—our unified platform for advancing trustworthy AI—we built the skill analyzer, a risk assessment engine designed to evaluate the safety and integrity of skills contributed by the community. However, throughout this process, we encountered a fundamental challenge: it proved impossible to uniquely identify a skill across sources, and verifying the true identity of a skill’s author was equally unfeasible. This realization led us to propose a new skill ID standard, aiming to provide a reliable, content-based identifier that can solve these identification and trust issues for AI skills.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo understand why, you need to know what a skill is. AI agents are extended through “skills” — directories containing a SKILL.md prompt file alongside supporting resources like scripts, documentation, and configuration. These skills live in GitHub repositories or skill marketplaces such as \u003ca href=\"https://skills.sh/\"\u003eskills.sh\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://ai.gendigital.com/\"\u003eGen ATH\u003c/a\u003e and ClawHub, and they work like plugins: install one and your AI agent gains new capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emy-skill/\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; SKILL.md\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# The prompt — what the agent does\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; lib/\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; helper.py\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # Supporting script\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; config.json\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # Configuration\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; templates/\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; output.md\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # Output template\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; README.md\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # Documentation\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen distributed, the directory gets packaged into a ZIP file. But the ZIP is transport packaging. The meaningful artifact is the directory tree and everything in it. A change to any file, any filename, any directory in the tree produces a different skill. You need to fingerprint the whole thing as a unit.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy a simple hash doesn’t work\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe obvious answer is “hash it.” SHA-256 the artifact and use the digest as an identifier. This works well for single files — the security industry has used PE hashes, package checksums and file digests for decades. But a skill is a directory, not a file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou could hash the ZIP. But ZIPs are non-deterministic. The same directory tree produces different ZIP files depending on which tool created them, what compression level was used, and when. ZIP metadata includes timestamps — if you ZIP the same files a minute apart, the bytes differ. Different ZIP libraries order entries differently. Some tools store modification timestamps to the second; others truncate to two-second granularity. The ZIP format itself allows multiple valid representations of the same content.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen there are cross-platform problems. A skill author on Windows produces paths with backslashes \u003ccode\u003e(lib\\helper.py)\u003c/code\u003e; on Unix, the same path uses forward slashes \u003ccode\u003e(lib/helper.py)\u003c/code\u003e. macOS HFS+ stores filenames in NFD Unicode normalization; Linux and Windows typically use NFC. The same accented character — é — might be one codepoint or two depending on the OS that created the ZIP.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eZIP packaging conventions vary too. Some tools wrap all files in a top-level directory named after the archive \u003ccode\u003e(my-skill/SKILL.md)\u003c/code\u003e; others dump files at the root (SKILL.md). Download the same skill from GitHub’s “Download ZIP” button and from a CI pipeline, and you’ll likely get different wrapper directory names — or no wrapper at all.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHashing the ZIP bytes gives you a transport identifier, not a content identifier. Two ZIPs containing byte-for-byte identical files will produce different hashes. The same skill, downloaded from two sources, will look like two different skills.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat we need is an identifier that represents the \u003cem\u003elogical content\u003c/em\u003e of the skill directory — one that changes if and only if the actual files or structure change.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=MVQ4inae 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=lsIe9jW5 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":638},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=rEds36Hw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=QZFMqgOY 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":485},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=tMdiMDFL 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=7AcJyNfL 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":376}],"caption":"Single binary hashing vs. skill directory hashing","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/binary-vs-skill-directory.jpg.webp?itok=tMdiMDFL","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eDesigning the Skill ID\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm draws on a well-tested idea: git’s tree hashing. Git solved the “fingerprint a directory tree” problem decades ago by hashing a sorted list of entries where each entry records the type, name and content hash of a file or subdirectory. We adapted this approach for skill packages with normalization steps that neutralize the differences between archive formats, packaging tools and operating systems.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere’s the algorithm, step by step.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e1. Extract the file tree from the archive\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eRead every entry in the archive — ZIP, tar.gz, tar.bz2, whatever the transport format happens to be. For each entry, record whether it’s a file or a directory and read the file content. Discard all archive metadata: timestamps, permissions, compression settings. Only the file tree survives.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e2. Normalize paths\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach path goes through four normalizations:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e21b88a9a5d5480e58e9d3c738b97270e\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBackslash to forward slash\u003c/strong\u003e —\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003elib\\helper.py becomes\u0026nbsp;lib/helper.py\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb5f9d1e35c2a3d65641ad7567333f0f7\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnicode NFC normalization\u003c/strong\u003e — the combining sequence\u0026nbsp;e +\u0026nbsp;\\\u003ccode\u003eu0301\u003c/code\u003e (NFD, common on macOS) becomes the precomposed\u0026nbsp;\\\u003ccode\u003eu00e9\u003c/code\u003e (NFC)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e163263142bf0c175cc6fbbdaef12dbc9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCollapse redundant separators and dot components\u003c/strong\u003e —\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003efoo//bar/./baz.txt becomes\u0026nbsp;foo/bar/baz.txt\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8d310b0b1c31492dad54af5f4609e78f\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStrip leading\u0026nbsp;./ and\u0026nbsp;/\u003c/strong\u003e —\u0026nbsp;./\u003ccode\u003eSKILL.md \u003c/code\u003ebecomes\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eSKILL.md\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter normalization, a file zipped on Windows with backslash paths produces the same normalized path as one zipped on Unix with forward slashes. A filename created on macOS in NFD matches the same filename created on Linux in NFC.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e3. Strip the wrapper directory\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf every entry lives under a single top-level directory (and there are no loose files at the root), strip that prefix. This handles the common case where packaging tools wrap contents in a directory named after the archive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA skill packaged as\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emy-skill/SKILL.md\u003c/code\u003e produces the same Skill ID as one packaged as\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eSKILL.md\u003c/code\u003e directly, or as\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003etotally-different-name/SKILL.md\u003c/code\u003e. The wrapper is a packaging artifact, not part of the skill’s identity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e4. Exclude the signature file\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemove any entry whose path is exactly\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eskill.sig\u003c/code\u003e. This file is reserved for the skill signature (more on that later) and must not participate in the Skill ID computation. The signature lives inside the skill directory but is invisible to the identity algorithm — the same principle as Authenticode’s excluded region in a PE binary.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e5. Hash each file\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eCompute the SHA-256 digest of each file’s raw content individually. Directory entries get the SHA-256 of empty bytes. These per-file hashes become useful later for caching.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e6. Build sorted tree entries\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eConstruct one entry per file and directory using null-byte delimiters:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;type\u0026gt;\\0\u0026lt;path\u0026gt;\\0\u0026lt;sha256_hex\u0026gt;\\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;type\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e is either\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003efile or\u0026nbsp;dir\u003c/code\u003e. Sort entries by path.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe null bytes prevent boundary collisions. Without them, a file named\u0026nbsp;ab with \u003ccode\u003ehash\u0026nbsp;cd\u003c/code\u003e... and a file named\u0026nbsp;a with \u003ccode\u003ehash\u0026nbsp;bcd\u003c/code\u003e... could produce the same concatenated string. The null delimiters make each field boundary unambiguous — same principle git uses with its tree entry format.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003e7. Hash the tree\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFeed the sorted, concatenated entries into a final SHA-256 hash. The resulting 64-character hex digest is the Skill ID.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere’s what the core logic looks like, stripped to its essentials:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cem\u003e# Exclude the signature file\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eentries\u0026nbsp;= [(p, d)\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003efor\u003c/strong\u003e p, d\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003ein\u003c/strong\u003e entries\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003eif\u003c/strong\u003e p\u0026nbsp;!=\u0026nbsp;\"skill.sig\"]\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003efile_hashes\u0026nbsp;= {}\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003etree_entries\u0026nbsp;= []\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cstrong\u003efor\u003c/strong\u003e path, is_dir\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003ein\u003c/strong\u003e entries:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003eif\u003c/strong\u003e is_dir:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; content_hash\u0026nbsp;= sha256(b\"\").hexdigest()\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; tree_entries.append((\"dir\", path, content_hash))\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003eelse\u003c/strong\u003e:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; content_hash\u0026nbsp;= sha256(file_contents[path]).hexdigest()\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; file_hashes[path]\u0026nbsp;= content_hash\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; tree_entries.append((\"file\", path, content_hash))\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cem\u003e# Sort by path for deterministic ordering\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003etree_entries.sort(key=\u003cstrong\u003elambda\u003c/strong\u003e e: e[1])\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cem\u003e# Hash the tree\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eskill_hasher\u0026nbsp;= sha256()\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u003cstrong\u003efor\u003c/strong\u003e entry_type, path, content_hash\u0026nbsp;\u003cstrong\u003ein\u003c/strong\u003e tree_entries:\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; line\u0026nbsp;=\u0026nbsp;f\"{entry_type}\\0{path}\\0{content_hash}\\n\".encode()\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; skill_hasher.update(line)\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eskill_id\u0026nbsp;= skill_hasher.hexdigest()\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe function returns both the Skill ID and the dictionary of per-file hashes. One call, two useful outputs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eA concrete example\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eA skill with two files — \u003ccode\u003eSKILL.md\u003c/code\u003e containing \u003ccode\u003e# My Skill\\nDo something useful.\\n\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003elib/helper.py containing def helper():\\n\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; return True\\n — produces Skill ID 8cc13e05527df88d6c53ab0534bf43aea3b8f7456ac135a0d78f859bc9e6bd85. Wrap those same files in my-skill/ and the ID is identical. Add a .DS_Store and it changes to 847c22d50591cfabeca26859f1295c903cd134e0b26cd146a9ea7283ad3241d0.\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=qSzr5Dd1 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=-OAoBS8E 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":638},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=YPIpQMqV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=R7AchVOi 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":485},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=TVM5pqP_ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=XtJEvV6V 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":376}],"caption":"Anatomy of a Skill ID — from directory tree to fingerprint","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/anatomy-of-skill-id.jpg.webp?itok=TVM5pqP_","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eProperties that matter\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Skill ID has one defining property: it changes if and only if meaningful content changes.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSame files, different archive timestamps? Same ID. Same files, different entry order in the archive? Same ID. Same files, wrapped in a directory called\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emy-skill/\u003c/code\u003e vs.\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003ebeta-release/\u003c/code\u003e vs.\u0026nbsp;no wrapper at all? Same ID. Windows backslashes vs.\u0026nbsp;Unix forward slashes? Same ID. macOS NFD filenames vs.\u0026nbsp;Linux NFC filenames? Same ID.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eChange one byte of one file? Different ID. Rename a file? Different ID. Add an empty file? Different ID. Change directory structure? Different ID.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe ID is deterministic — same input, same output. It’s content-addressable, derived solely from the files themselves. And it inherits SHA-256’s collision resistance.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne deliberate design choice: OS metadata files like\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e.DS_Store\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eThumbs.db\u003c/code\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e, and\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e__MACOSX/\u003c/code\u003e resource forks are \u003cem\u003eincluded\u003c/em\u003e in the hash, not filtered out. This is the conservative stance — defense over convenience. If a\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003e.DS_Store\u003c/code\u003e file appears inside a skill ZIP, the Skill ID reflects it. The alternative — silently ignoring certain filenames — introduces a filtering policy that would need ongoing maintenance and could mask unintended content. If a skill author’s build process leaks OS metadata into the ZIP, the Skill ID will catch it. That seems preferable to pretending those files don’t exist.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat you can build with a stable ID\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA content-addressable identifier for skills unlocks the same workflows that file hashes enabled for executables, and the security industry has been refining those workflows for decades.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAllowlisting\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eHash-based allowlisting is one of the oldest patterns in endpoint security: compute the hash of a known-good binary, store it and skip re-scanning when the same hash appears again. Skill IDs enable the same pattern. When a skill has been manually reviewed and cleared — say, a first-party office document conversion skill that happens to compile C code at runtime — its Skill ID goes into an override table. Any future submission matching that ID gets the pre-approved result immediately, no analysis pipeline needed. Change one byte and the override no longer applies, forcing a fresh review.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eContent-addressable storage\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Skill ID serves as a natural storage key. Upload a skill ZIP to object storage keyed by its Skill ID, and you get automatic deduplication — the same skill submitted by a hundred users is stored once. This is the same principle behind git objects and Docker image layers: address content by its hash, store it once, reference it everywhere.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePer-file caching\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe per-file hashes returned alongside the Skill ID enable granular caching of expensive analysis steps. An LLM-based security analysis of a single file might cost real money and real time. If a skill is updated but only one file changes, the per-file hashes let you reuse cached analysis for every unchanged file and re-analyze only the delta. The composite Skill ID changes (because one file changed), but most of the analysis work is already done.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDetection flagging\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIndividual file hashes connect directly to existing reputation and detection systems. If the\u0026nbsp;SKILL.md file in a skill contains references to known-malicious URLs, you can set a detection flag on that file’s SHA-256 hash — the same killbit mechanism the security industry uses for flagging malicious executables. The file hash becomes the bridge between the skill analysis system and established threat intelligence infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eVersion tracking\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery content change produces a new Skill ID. This creates natural version boundaries without requiring any explicit versioning scheme. If the ID is the same, the skill hasn’t changed. If it differs from what you saw last time, something is different — no version\u003ca\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/a\u003e\u003ca href=\"//7233A19B-A4BD-4ADF-A339-3B79CEBBCA6C#_msocom_1\" id=\"_anchor_1\"\u003e[AR1]\u003c/a\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"//7233A19B-A4BD-4ADF-A339-3B79CEBBCA6C#_msocom_2\" id=\"_anchor_2\"\u003e[MS2]\u003c/a\u003e\u0026nbsp;parsing, no changelog inspection, no trust in a self-reported version number. The content itself is the version.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe parallel to traditional security is direct. PE files get a SHA-256 hash. NuGet and npm packages get checksums. Container images get content digests. Skill IDs serve the same role for a new artifact type — one that happens to be a directory tree instead of a single file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow we use it\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Skill ID gates our analysis pipeline at Gen Threat Labs. Same ID means cached results — skip everything. New ID triggers a fresh analysis pass: static content extraction, URL reputation checks, AV scanning and AI-powered security review.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=Z4G2GnzN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=318N6QEo 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":638},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=nQ7dUYbP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=XVpEWy_t 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":485},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=g7JxeRNY 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=mYOA2tew 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":376}],"caption":"Skill analysis pipeline — Skill ID gates the intake","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/analysis-pipeline.jpg.webp?itok=g7JxeRNY","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eSigning skills: From identity to authenticity\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Skill ID answers “what is this skill?” — but not “who published it?” or “has it been tampered with since publication?” Identity is necessary but not sufficient. We also need authenticity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe approach borrows directly from Authenticode signing of Windows PE files. In a PE binary, the Authenticode signature is embedded inside the file itself, in a region that the hash algorithm knows to skip. The signature is physically present in the file but excluded from the hash calculation. This lets the binary carry its own proof of authenticity without that proof interfering with its identity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe skill equivalent is the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eskill.sig\u003c/code\u003e file, which lives inside the skill directory but the Skill ID algorithm skips it (step 4 above). Same principle, different artifact type.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe\u0026nbsp;skill.sig file uses a CMS SignedData envelope (PKCS #7 / RFC 5652), the same signature format Authenticode uses. The envelope contains the digital signature, the signer’s full certificate chain (end-entity certificate through intermediate CAs), and optionally an RFC 3161 timestamp countersignature from a trusted Timestamping Authority. The signed content isn’t the bare Skill ID but a versioned payload string:\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eSKILL_ID:1:sha256:\u0026lt;skill_id\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e. The version and algorithm fields provide forward compatibility; if the digest algorithm or normalization rules ever change, the verifier knows exactly which rules produced the enclosed ID.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEmbedding the full certificate chain is what makes the skill self-verifiable. The signature, the chain, and the identity all travel together in the same artifact. No external key lookup is needed at verification time. A marketplace can verify on ingest. An agent runtime can verify before execution. An enterprise security team can verify before deployment.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eVerification follows the same pattern as Authenticode: compute the Skill ID while excluding\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eskill.sig\u003c/code\u003e, parse the CMS envelope, confirm the signed payload matches the computed ID, build the certificate chain up to a trusted root, validate expiry and the code-signing Extended Key Usage, check revocation via CRL or OCSP and verify the cryptographic signature. If a timestamp countersignature is present and the signer’s certificate has expired but was valid at the countersigned time, the signature is still accepted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pattern enables several trust models. Author signing: the skill author obtains a code-signing certificate and signs the Skill ID, proving authorship. Marketplace attestation: a marketplace countersigns skill IDs it has vetted, adding a layer of review. Private CA: enterprises operate their own certificate authority and issue signing certificates to approved authors, restricting execution to internally-approved skills. Revocation: when a signing key is compromised, the CA publishes the certificate on its CRL, and verifiers reject all skills signed with that certificate unless a valid timestamp countersignature proves the signature predates the revocation. Each model builds on the same primitive: a CMS envelope over the signed payload, excluded from the ID itself.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLooking ahead\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA stable, cross-platform identifier for skills opens several directions worth pursuing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSHA-256 hashes are the lingua franca of malware intelligence — any vendor, any platform, same hash for the same file. Skill IDs could serve the same role for skill reputation. A skill flagged as malicious on one marketplace could be recognized on another or by an independent scanning service, using nothing but the Skill ID. No API integration required.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe per-file hashes already make incremental analysis possible in principle. When a skill is updated, diff the per-file hash dictionaries: unchanged hashes reuse cached analysis, changed hashes trigger re-analysis. For LLM-based analysis (the most expensive stage in the pipeline) this could cut costs and latency substantially on minor updates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSkill ID is a companion to\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/news/company-news/ai-agent-runtime-security\"\u003eAARTS\u003c/a\u003e, our standard for runtime agent action transparency. AARTS defines how agent actions are intercepted and evaluated at runtime; Skill ID defines how skills are identified and authenticated before they’re loaded. Together they cover both runtime behavior and supply chain identity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eConclusion\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSkills are code — natural-language code, but code nonetheless. And like all code that gets distributed, shared, and executed, skills need an identity before the security industry’s established workflows can apply to them.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Skill ID is deliberately unoriginal. SHA-256 for content hashing. Git-style tree entries for directory fingerprinting. Null-byte delimiters for boundary safety. Path normalization for cross-platform consistency. Wrapper stripping for transport independence. Every piece has been proven elsewhere; we assembled them for a new artifact type.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe value isn’t in the hash. It’s in what the hash enables: allowlisting, deduplication, per-file caching, detection flagging, version tracking and cryptographic signing. These are the same workflows the security industry built around file hashes over the past two decades. Skills just needed their own version of the fingerprint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMarketplace operators can implement Skill ID for verifiable identity; agent runtimes can verify IDs before loading skills. The spec and reference implementation are on\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://github.com/gendigitalinc/skill-id-standard\"\u003eGitHub\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs part of the broader Agent Trust Hub vision, Skill ID lays the groundwork for an AI ecosystem where skills can be identified, authenticated and governed with the same rigor we expect from every other critical software layer.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"18","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-03/ChatGPT%20Image%20Mar%2017%2C%202026%2C%2010_28_44%20AM.jpg?h=197483aa\u0026itok=1GIB-EAP","language":"en","heroImage":{"default":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_hero_extra_wide/public/2026-03/ChatGPT%20Image%20Mar%2017%2C%202026%2C%2010_28_44%20AM.jpg.webp?h=197483aa\u0026itok=-6deULxw Threat Hunting is how we use AI to dramatically accelerate threat investigations without giving up rigor. Senior researchers stay in control, but they gain a force multiplier, like having extra analysts available on demand. We build this with custom prompts, repeatable investigation playbooks and tool-connected agent workflows that keep work consistent and auditable. The result is faster understanding, faster response and better protection for users.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn a recent investigation, this approach produced a complete, publishable, evidence-backed analysis about \u003cstrong\u003e30 times faster\u003c/strong\u003e than a traditional workflow, without skipping verification.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’re building this as a repeatable capability across investigations, not an ad hoc trick.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIntroducing ‘Agentic Threat Hunting’\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers scale by default. They automate, iterate and copy what works until defenders are forced into a permanent reaction cycle.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders don’t lose because they lack intelligence or tools. Defenders lose because time is finite.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThreat hunting is not a single breakthrough moment. It’s a chain of steps and the chain breaks where time and attention run out.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDeep technical analysis is still one of the highest leverage things a security team can do. It is also one of the hardest things to scale, because it depends on expert attention and takes a great deal of time. A strong researcher can only do so many hours of careful extraction, correlation, hypothesis testing, and write-up in a day.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat bottleneck is changing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNot because AI “does the research,” but because \u003cstrong\u003eagentic workflows compress time\u003c/strong\u003e. With the right operating model, a senior analyst can direct AI agents like a small team of junior researchers. Multiple threads run in parallel, while the human stays responsible for judgement, verification, and final conclusions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what we mean by \u003cstrong\u003eAgentic Threat Hunting\u003c/strong\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not “using a chatbot sometimes”. It is a new operating model for how threat hunting gets done, and at Gen we are treating it like a capability we are building, evolving, and systematizing internally.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe problem we are solving: time is the limiting factor\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eA modern threat investigation rarely arrives as one tidy unit of work. It arrives in pieces, a suspicious sample, related variants, infrastructure breadcrumbs, partial configuration and a growing set of “maybe” links that need to be proven or discarded.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEven the best researchers lose days to work that is necessary but doesn’t deserve senior attention:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4d4fc4da96a97bab25024c23b8d762d\"\u003eRepeating extraction across variants\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e73acdd465dbd96036cddd48f4c40f2fd\"\u003eOrganizing artifacts into a structure that can be compared and revisited\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e645e7385bdb99bb596fa419cf683f219\"\u003eMaintaining multiple hypothesis threads without mixing assumptions into facts\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e147fad2170c65380f9fb546d79fc7971\"\u003eTurning raw technical detail into outputs that other teams can act on\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe cost is not just speed. When time gets tight, risk increases. Steps get shortened, context gets lost and important connections can be missed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the gap Agentic Threat Hunting is designed to close.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat changes: parallel work, one accountable owner\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe difference with Agentic Threat Hunting is not that the model “finds the truth”. It’s that a senior researcher can run multiple investigation threads in parallel, without losing the plot.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of one person doing everything serially, agents can take on defined roles that mirror how a small team works:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e15efe13406c7eea8f2e84b2546de554f\"\u003eone agent structures artifacts into consistent notes and a capability map,\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2c946dfdb49a47a9370eb7cd27a615c3\"\u003eanother maintains a living hypothesis tree, highlighting contradictions and what still needs proof,\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e95bbc6816e09d0285fd36c7c45d29c22\"\u003eanother drafts evidence-linked summaries for internal stakeholders while the researcher keeps investigating.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe senior researcher becomes the director, the reviewer and the owner of every conclusion. The work scales, but accountability does not move.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat stays the same: credibility, traceability and ownership of claims\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf we only talk about speed, this turns into hype.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI can sound confident while being wrong. That is not a theoretical risk, it is the default failure mode of careless use. So the core principle of Agentic Threat Hunting is simple:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAgents can accelerate work, but researchers own the claims.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn practice, that means evidence remains the source of truth, not the agent’s narrative. Every meaningful conclusion must be traceable back to artifacts and anything that cannot be traced stays a hypothesis. Confident language is not proof. Verification is not optional. Humans remain accountable for what we ship internally and what we publish externally.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is how you get the upside of speed without sacrificing trustworthiness.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eHow it works in practice: two examples\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eExample 1: Variant wrangling without losing rigor\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMalware rarely arrives as a single, well-defined specimen. It arrives as a stream of variants, each with small changes that matter.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn a traditional workflow, analysts repeat the same extraction steps across variants, then try to keep the differences straight across notes, screenshots and half-finished write-ups.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith an agentic workflow, we can delegate the repetitive passes. Agents produce consistent, structured summaries of behaviors, configuration patterns and meaningful deltas across samples. The senior researcher then focuses on the high-value part: verifying the key claims, spotting what changed and deciding what those changes imply.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eExample 2: From raw technical detail to outputs people can actually use\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEven when the analysis is correct, a lot of time disappears translating it into something others can act on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile the researcher is validating evidence, agents can draft two versions of the same findings, one technical for internal response teams (actionable, precise, evidence-linked), and one plain-language for a broader audience (clear, non-technical, no unnecessary detail).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe human still owns the narrative, edits for accuracy and decides what is publishable, but translation stops being the limiting factor.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe engine behind it: playbooks, prompts and roles that work like a team\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe difference between “we tried AI” and “we are building Agentic Threat Hunting” is discipline.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe are not relying on generic prompts or one-off interactions. We develop and refine prompts and playbooks that reflect how investigations actually work, including how we structure evidence, how we express uncertainty, how we separate “observed” from “inferred” and how we keep conclusions traceable back to artifacts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen we operationalize that into agent roles, so the work is repeatable instead of reinventing every time.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost importantly, this is not static. We continuously evolve the approach, creating new ways to take advantage of agents while keeping the same standard of rigor. That continuous evolution matters, because threat hunting is adversarial work. Attackers adapt, and tooling must adapt too.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhy it matters: faster investigations mean better protection\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers will use the same force multipliers. Agentic workflows can reduce their cost of experimentation, speed up iteration and help scale campaigns that used to require more manual effort.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly why we’re investing in Agentic Threat Hunting as a disciplined capability, not a novelty. The advantage will not come from “having models.” It will come from having better workflows, stronger verification habits and faster learning loops.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the investigative cycle is shorter, detections improve sooner, infrastructure can be identified and disrupted sooner, response teams get clarity sooner and users are protected sooner.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is a second-order effect too. Senior researchers spend less time doing necessary but repetitive wor and more time doing what makes them senior: connecting dots, spotting patterns, and making high-stakes judgment calls.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words, AI compresses time, but it does not compress responsibility. That stays How LLMs Are Mass-Producing Disposable Stage 1 Loaders ","created":"1773427225","subtitle":" How malware authors use LLM‑driven “promptmorphism” to endlessly reshape loaders—and what defenders must do authors have always understood a simple truth: defenders scale by recognizing patterns, attackers buy time by breaking them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor years, that meant packers, obfuscation, and classic polymorphism. In many campaigns, server-side polymorphism ensured each target received a slightly different sample, enough to blur signatures and slow down clustering, while the real investment (later-stage payloads, infrastructure, monetization) stayed steady.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow, we are seeing a modern version of the same strategy: \u003cstrong\u003ePromptmorphism\u003c/strong\u003e, the rapid regeneration of stage 1 loaders into endless fresh-looking variants. In practice, this is the loader treated as a disposable wrapper, continuously rewritten to stay ahead of similarity-based detection. In simple terms, this means attackers no longer rely only on traditional code mutation. Instead, they can use AI prompts to repeatedly generate new versions of the same loader, creating a steady stream of slightly different files that all perform the same job.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo be clear, not every fast-changing loader is LLM-written, some are driven by template engines, build pipelines, or manual refactors. What has changed is how easy LLMs make high-frequency rewrites at scale, especially for small, modular first-stage components.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat we mean by Promptmorphism\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003ePromptmorphism is LLM-assisted code variation used to continuously regenerate the early-stage loader, producing many distinct implementations of the same minimal job. Because LLMs are probabilistic models, unless outputs are tightly constrained, small prompt or sampling changes naturally produce different implementations of the same logic, variation becomes the default rather than the exception.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 1 rarely needs to be clever. It needs to run, perform a few checks, then fetch, decrypt, unpack, or hand off to stage 2. That narrow scope makes it ideal for rapid rewriting. An LLM can re-express the same logic with different structure, different API usage, different control flow, and different naming conventions, without changing the campaign’s underlying playbook.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a defender’s perspective, this matters because stage 1 is what you encounter first. It is the component most likely to be scanned, sandboxed, executed, and shared. If that component can be churned endlessly, workflows that rely heavily on static similarity and quick clustering lose leverage.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=eUJtty16 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=EW93ZqdR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":782},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=3VFPbDaY 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=-ezctoP8 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":595},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=rAy1IJE- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=5Faf3WlC 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":461}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/Untitled%20design%20%287%29.jpg.webp?itok=rAy1IJE-","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch3\u003eA real-world example: a LaaS “packaging roulette”\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo make this concrete, here’s what this looks like in the wild.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe are tracking a \u003cstrong\u003eloader-as-a-service (LaaS)\u003c/strong\u003e ecosystem where multiple campaigns rely on a shared loader that is frequently updated and redistributed. We have seen the same loader layer used to drop different malware, including Wincir and Stealc.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat is striking is not just that the loader changes; it is how fast it changes. Over a short span of days, we observed the same stage 1 job implemented with a rotating set of packaging choices:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2726bbacc59a47e7cc5196151e3632ce\"\u003eEncrypted payload embedded in the executable, with keying material present alongside it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e243bca6bd708acaf9e1516c7570fcf0b\"\u003eA shift to hex-encoded payloads.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3d909c138c86b09a0626deebf3d97cb1\"\u003eThen multiple hex-encoded chunks (for example, 10 parts) that had to be assembled at runtime.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec02244e38543f8cc10f1e0dd4ee3fedf\"\u003eA return to raw encrypted bytes embedded directly in the executable.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ece89b03e3f39e29ef7e799c7cbb858e4\"\u003eMoving encrypted bytes into the PE overlay (data appended beyond the formal end of the PE image).\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecd6c43a00f4894fe776d49e8ad394d0a\"\u003ePlacing encrypted bytes inside x64 code itself, effectively blending “data” into executable regions.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e018a914e3e8d096c98a97e155fc5169b\"\u003eIn at least one iteration, swapping cryptography (for example, ChaCha instead of AES).\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe key takeaway is that the mission stays broadly the same, but the wrapper changes repeatedly. If you have ever dealt with server-side polymorphism, this will feel familiar. The difference is that the source of variation is no longer only classic packing and obfuscation transformations\u0026nbsp;and encryption wrappers. Increasingly, the operational goal is achieved via rapid rewrite cycles, whether driven by LLM assistance, aggressive refactoring, or automated build pipelines designed to mutate the first stage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhy stage 1 is the perfect target for this approach\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 1 is exposed. It has to survive the widest range of defensive controls (gateway scanning, reputation systems, endpoint scanning, sandboxing). It also tends to be modular, which makes it easier to swap.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the Wincir chain, analysis shows a loader that includes multiple capabilities common in modern staging:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5fedfdbf81ef4bb4cf1218021ba4abac\"\u003edecryption and decompression routines\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb63f7db44a54416ae48d419d8029644b\"\u003eAPI resolution and loader scaffolding\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9c690a2461baf1b5e0ee4b6f58858a31\"\u003einjection and execution plumbing\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e88eab72f7c1378e9b9da2753bd9c398a\"\u003eanti-analysis checks\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e735a9629325ff7a9e68b8e72cbb95d14\"\u003enetwork retrieval for follow-on components\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecee60024174fd37c8eb6bc0428b685b0\"\u003ein some samples, script-host execution paths (for example via Windows script engines)\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is exactly why attackers focus mutation here. If stage 1 buys them a few extra hours or days before defenders build durable clustering and detections, the rest of the chain gets to run.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eHow Promptmorphism differs from classic server-side polymorphism\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is tempting to label all of this “polymorphism,” but there is a meaningful difference between transforming an artifact and re-authoring one.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4193c5eef015653a5ab33dd042ade53\"\u003eServer-side polymorphism often starts with the same logic and changes representation (packing, encryption layers, stub transformations).\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e26b4d8f2ec6385e877ed0425fda2bd25\"\u003ePromptmorphism can change the implementation itself, reorganizing code structure, swapping APIs, rewriting helper logic, and reshaping control flow, while preserving behavior.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn practice, this can reduce obvious byte-level similarity and break simple clustering. At the same time, it introduces new seams: if attackers lean on the same generation patterns, prompts, or scaffolding, they can accidentally create a different kind of fingerprint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe attacker tradeoffs, this is not magic\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is useful to be skeptical of the “AI makes malware unstoppable” framing. Promptmorphism creates pressure, but it also creates friction for attackers:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7970a7f1f532c818c34e57e1a6d7b097\"\u003eQuality varies, rewrites introduce bugs.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ebfca458e3a3ea82f9b3b8288f822abbf\"\u003eConsistency drops, which can hurt reliability across environments.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e14de79ac7b6f36d18b65ec06c536ca66\"\u003eNoise increases, rewritten code often adds unnecessary behavior or sloppy error paths.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec3611852372e6b9d6483291368ede1b9\"\u003eStable anchors become more important, because defenders shift focus to what does not change.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eA useful way to frame it is this: Promptmorphism shifts the battleground. It does not eliminate detection; it pressures defenders to pivot from “what does this file look like” to “what does this campaign do.”\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eHow defenders should respond when stage 1 becomes disposable\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the loader is unstable, the durable signals are usually elsewhere:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec5edafdd9bd6401a501b9f57ae0100a4\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBehavioral anchors:\u003c/strong\u003e process tree patterns, persistence choices, execution handoff, network call sequences.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eac0d9ff58060a011c47a3815ad79af25\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCampaign correlation:\u003c/strong\u003e infrastructure reuse, endpoint patterns, TLS/HTTP fingerprints, timing and targeting patterns.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e091d6a05e49e8a129e05c5b63f1d3054\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eChokepoints:\u003c/strong\u003e second-stage retrieval, config formats, decryption and decompression routines that remain consistent across waves.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e93d996af05db9dc6fe891e023cd1d093\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDistribution signals:\u003c/strong\u003e delivery channels, lures, attachment styles, redirect chains.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 1 can be rewritten. The campaign economics, infrastructure constraints, and operational habits are harder to rewrite every day.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eClosing Thought\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003ePromptmorphism is not just “AI malware.” It is a sign of industrialization.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers have always chased cheap novelty. Promptmorphism is a new production method for an old business goal: keep the front door unstable long enough to get inside.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDefenders can respond by refusing to treat stage 1 as the “identity” of the threat. When the loader becomes disposable, the campaign becomes the unit of analysis, and that is where durable defenses and durable disruptions are won.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-03/Picture1.jpg?h=dab30fcb\u0026itok=pSJoY7sg","language":"en","heroImage":{"default":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_hero_extra_wide/public/2026-03/Picture1.jpg.webp?h=dab30fcb\u0026itok=xOsYIt1N
